\textstyle{\langle 1,3\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{3}_{\mathcal{Q}}}
\textstyle{\langle 2,4\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Now let us consider a quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
of twisted type; i.e,
\mathfrak{g}=A_{2n-1}^{(2)},A_{2n}^{(2)},D_{n+1}^{(2)}
,
E_{6}^{(2)}

and
D_{4}^{(3)}
.
For each
twisted
quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}^{(t)})

(t=2,3)
, we assign the finite simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{fin}}}
of symmetric type as follows:

\displaystyle\small\begin{array}[]{|c||c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\mathfrak{g}&A_{2n-1}^{(2)}\ (n\geq 2)&A_{2n}^{(2)}\ (n\geq 1)&D_{n+1}^{(2)}\ (n\geq 3)&E_{6}^{(2)}&D_{4}^{(3)}\\
\hline\cr\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{fin}}&A_{2n-1}&A_{2n}&D_{n+1}&E_{6}&D_{4}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Then we take
{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{Q}
for
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}^{(t)})

(t=2,3)
as the ones of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}^{(1)})
.

1.5.
Subcategories

For a quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
of untwisted type
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
, we can choose a
\mathrm{Q}
-datum
\mathcal{Q}=(\blacktriangle,\sigma,\xi)
as seen in the previous subsection.

For each
(\imath,p)\in\blacktriangle_{0}\times\mathbb{Z}
, we set

\mathcal{V}^{(1)}(\imath,p)\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}V(\varpi_{\imath})_{(-q)^{p}}&\text{if $\mathfrak{g}$ is of simply-laced affine type},\\
V(\varpi_{\overline{\imath}})_{(-1)^{n+{\overline{\imath}}}q_{\mathfrak{s}}^{p}}&\text{if $\mathfrak{g}$ is of type $B^{(1)}_{n}$},\\
V(\varpi_{\overline{\imath}})_{(-q_{\mathfrak{s}})^{p}}&\text{if $\mathfrak{g}$ is of type $C^{(1)}_{n}$},\\
V(\varpi_{\overline{\imath}})_{(-1)^{i+p}q_{\mathfrak{s}}^{p}}&\text{if $\mathfrak{g}$ is of type $F^{(1)}_{4}$},\\
V(\varpi_{\overline{\imath}})_{(-q_{\mathfrak{t}})^{p}}&\text{if $\mathfrak{g}$ is of type $G^{(1)}_{2}$},\end{cases}\quad\text{where $q=q_{\mathfrak{s}}^{2}=q_{\mathfrak{t}}^{3}$.}

For a quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
of twisted type
\mathfrak{g}^{(t)}

(t=2,3)
and

(\imath,p)\in\blacktriangle_{0}\times\mathbb{Z}
, we assign the fundamental module
\mathcal{V}^{(t)}(\imath,p)

(t=2,3)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(t)}(\imath,p)\mathbin{:=}V(\varpi_{\pi(\imath)})_{A(\imath,p)},

where

\displaystyle\pi(\imath)\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}\imath&\text{ if }{\rm(i)}\ \mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=A^{(2)}_{N}\text{ and }\imath\leq\lceil N/2\rceil\text{ or }{\rm(ii)}\ \mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=D^{(2)}_{n+1}\text{ and }\imath<n,\\[4.30554pt]
N+1-\imath&\text{ if }\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=A^{(2)}_{N}\text{ and }\imath>\lceil N/2\rceil,\\
n&\text{ if }\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=D^{(2)}_{n+1}\text{ and }\imath=n,n+1,\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\pi(1)=\pi(6)=1,\quad\pi(3)=\pi(5)=2,\quad\pi(4)=3,\quad\pi(2)=4&\text{if $\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=E_{6}^{(2)}$},\\
\pi(1)=\pi(3)=\pi(4)=1,\quad\pi(2)=2&\text{if
$\mathfrak{g}^{(3)}=D_{4}^{(3)}$,}\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle A(\imath,p)\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}(-q)^{p}&\text{if }{\rm(i)}\ \mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=A^{(2)}_{N}\text{ and }\imath\leq\lceil N/2\rceil\\
&\quad\text{ or }{\rm(ii)}\ \mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=E^{(2)}_{6}\text{ and }\imath=1,3,\\
(-1)^{N}(-q)^{p}&\text{if }\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=A^{(2)}_{N}\text{ and }\imath>\lceil N/2\rceil,\\
(\sqrt{-1})^{n+1-\imath}(-q)^{p}&\text{if }\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=D^{(2)}_{n+1}\text{ and }\imath<n,\\
(-1)^{\imath}(-q)^{p}&\text{if }\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=D^{(2)}_{n+1}\text{ and }\imath=n,n+1,\\
-(-q)^{p}&\text{if }\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=E^{(2)}_{6}\text{ and }\imath=5,6,\\
(\sqrt{-1})(-q)^{p}&\text{if }\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=E^{(2)}_{6}\text{ and }\imath=2,4,\\
\bigl(\delta_{\imath,1}-\delta_{\imath,2}+\delta_{\imath,3}\omega+\delta_{\imath,4}\omega^{2}\bigr)(-q)^{p}&\text{if
$\mathfrak{g}^{(3)}=D^{(3)}_{4}$.}\end{cases}

Here
\omega
is the third root of unity.

For each quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}^{(t)})

(t=1,2,3)
, its
\mathrm{Q}
-datum
\mathcal{Q}=(\blacktriangle,\sigma,\xi)
and
(\beta,k)\in\Phi^{+}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{fin}}}\times\mathbb{Z}
with
\phi^{-1}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta,k)=(\imath,p)\in{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}_{0}
, we define

\mathcal{V}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta,k)\mathbin{:=}\mathcal{V}^{(t)}(\imath,p).

For notational simplicity, we usually write
\mathcal{V}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta)
instead of
\mathcal{V}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta,0)
if
k=0
,
and drop
(t)
if there is no danger of confusion.

Note that, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\mathcal{V}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta,k)=\mathscr{D}^{k}\mathcal{V}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta).

Definition 1.7

(
[
HL15
,
HL16
,
KKKO15b
,
KO18
,
OS19b
]
)
.

Let
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}^{(t)})
be a quantum affine algebra
(t=1,2
or
3)
.

(i)

We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
the smallest abelian full subcategory of the category
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}

(a)

it is stable under subquotient, tensor product and extension,

(b)

it contains
\mathcal{V}^{(t)}(\imath,k)
for all
(\imath,k)\in{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}_{0}
.

Note that the definition of
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
does not depend on the choice of
\mathcal{Q}
.

(ii)

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
\mathscr{C}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}[k]
the smallest abelian full subcategory of the category
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{(t)}}
such that

(a)

it is stable under subquotient, tensor product and extension,

(b)

it contains
\mathscr{D}^{k}\mathcal{V}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta)
for all
\beta\in\Phi^{+}_{\mathsf{g}}
, and the trivial module
\mathbf{1}
.

When
k=0
, we write
\mathscr{C}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}
instead of
\mathscr{C}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}[0]
for simplicity.
We call
\mathscr{C}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}[k]
a
heart
subcategory of
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(iii)

For
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
\mathscr{C}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q},\leq m}

the smallest abelian full subcategory of the category
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}

(a)

it is stable under subquotient, tensor product and extension,

(b)

it contains
\mathscr{D}^{s}\mathcal{V}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta)
for all
\beta\in\Phi^{+}_{\mathsf{g}}
and
s\leq m
.

Similarly, we can define
\mathscr{C}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q},<m}
,
\mathscr{C}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q},\geq m}
and
\mathscr{C}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q},>m}
, respectively.

Remark 1.8
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
by considering all fundamental modules
\{\mathcal{V}(\imath_{s},p_{s})\ |\ 1\leq s\leq\ell\}
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{Q}}[k]
, the full subquiver of

{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}
with vertices
\{(\imath_{s},p_{s})\ |\ 1\leq s\leq\ell\}
is denoted by
\Gamma^{\mathcal{Q}}[k]
. The quiver

\Gamma^{\mathcal{Q}}[k]\simeq\Gamma^{\mathcal{Q}}
as quivers,
\Gamma^{\mathcal{Q}}[k]\cap\Gamma^{\mathcal{Q}}[l]=\emptyset
unless
k=l
, and

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(\Gamma^{\mathcal{Q}}[k])_{0}={\widehat{\blacktriangle}}_{0}
.

These categories for simply-laced untwisted affine type
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
were introduced in
[
HL10
,
HL15
]
.
Note that
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is a skeleton category and every skeleton category can be obtained from

\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
by taking a parameter shifts.
The Grothendieck ring
K(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}})
of
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is isomorphic to the
commutative
polynomial ring generated by
the classes of modules in
\{\mathscr{D}^{k}\mathcal{V}^{(t)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta)\ |\ \beta\in\Phi_{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{fin}}}^{+},\ k\in\mathbb{Z}\}

[
FR99
]
.

Proposition 1.9

(
[
KKOP24c
, Proposition 5.4]
)
.

For
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|m-n|>1
,
if a simple module
M
is contained in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{Q}}[m]\cap\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{Q}}[n]
, then
M\simeq\mathbf{1}
.

For a fundamental module
V\in\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, there exists an
(\imath,p)
such that
V\simeq\mathcal{V}(\imath,p)
. We set

{\rm clr}(V):=\imath

and call it the
color
of
V
.
Since we fix the parity function
\epsilon
, the color of
V
is well-defined.

Example 1.10
.

Using (
1.22
), we can see that

\mathrm{clr}(V(\varpi_{2})_{(-1)q_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-2}})=4\quad\text{{and}}\quad\mathrm{clr}(V(\varpi_{2})_{(-1)q_{\mathfrak{s}}^{4}})=2,

since
V(\varpi_{2})_{(-1)q_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-2}}=\mathcal{V}(4,-2)
and
V(\varpi_{2})_{(-1)q_{\mathfrak{s}}^{4}}=\mathcal{V}(2,4)
.

2.
R
-matrices and
\mathbb{Z}
-invariants

In this section, we recall the
R
-matrix in the representation theory of quantum affine algebra and
\mathbb{Z}
-invariants arising from
R
-matrix, in which the latter is introduced in
[
KKOP20
]
.

2.1.
R
-matrices

In this subsection, we review the notion of
R
-matrices on
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules and their coefficients
by following
[
Kas02
, §8]
,
[
AK97
, Appendices A and B]
and
[
KKOP20
]
mainly.

For modules
M
and
N\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, there exists
\mathbf{k}(\hskip-1.72218pt(z)\hskip-1.72218pt)\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-module isomorphism

R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}\colon\mathbf{k}(\hskip-1.72218pt(z)\hskip-1.72218pt)\mathop{\otimes}_{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]}(M\mathop{\otimes}N_{z})\to\mathbf{k}(\hskip-1.72218pt(z)\hskip-1.72218pt)\mathop{\otimes}_{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]}(N_{z}\mathop{\otimes}M)

satisfying

\textstyle{\mathbf{k}(\!(z)\!)\mathop{\otimes}_{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]}(M\mathop{\otimes}N\mathop{\otimes}L_{z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M\mathop{\otimes}R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{N,L_{z}}}
\scriptstyle{R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M\mathop{\otimes}N,L_{z}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{k}(\!(z)\!)\mathop{\otimes}_{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]}(L_{z}\mathop{\otimes}M\mathop{\otimes}N)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{k}(\!(z)\!)\mathop{\otimes}_{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]}(M\mathop{\otimes}L_{z}\mathop{\otimes}N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,L_{z}}\mathop{\otimes}N}

for
L,M,N\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and further properties (see
[
Kas02
]
for more details). We call
R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}
the
universal
R
-matrix
of
M
and
N
.

For modules
M
and
N\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we say that
R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}
is
rationally renormalizable
if there exists
c_{M,N}(z)\in\mathbf{k}(\hskip-1.72218pt(z)\hskip-1.72218pt)^{\times}

such that
c_{M,N}(z)
is unique up to a multiple of
\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]^{\times}
,

(i)

R^{\mathrm{ren}}_{M,N_{z}}\mathbin{:=}c_{M,N}(z)R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}\colon M\otimes N_{z}\to N_{z}\otimes M
and

(ii)

R^{\mathrm{ren}}_{M,N_{z}}|_{z=x}
does not vanish for any
x\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
.

We call
c_{M,N}(z)
the
renormalizing coefficient
.
In the case, we write

{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{M,N}}$}}\mathbin{:=}R^{\mathrm{ren}}_{M,N_{z}}|_{z=1}
and call it
R
-matrix
.

By the definition,
{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{M,N}}$}}
never vanishes.

Now assume that
M
and
N
are non-zero simple
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then the universal
R
-matrix
R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}
is rationally renormalizable.
Furthermore, we have the following:
Let
u
and
v
be dominant extremal weight vectors of
M
and
N
, respectively.
Then there exists
a_{M,N}(z)\in\mathbf{k}[[z]]^{\times}
such that

(2.5)

\displaystyle R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}\big(u\mathop{\otimes}v_{z}\big)=a_{M,N}(z)\big(v_{z}\mathop{\otimes}u\big).

Then
R^{\mathrm{norm}}_{M,N_{z}}\mathbin{:=}a_{M,N}(z)^{-1}R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}
is a unique
\mathbf{k}(z)\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-module isomorphism

R^{\mathrm{norm}}_{M,N_{z}}\colon\mathbf{k}(z)\otimes_{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]}\big(M\otimes N_{z}\big)\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{k}(z)\otimes_{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]}\big(N_{z}\otimes M\big)

satisfying

R^{\mathrm{norm}}_{M,N}\big(u\otimes v_{z}\big)=v_{z}\otimes u.

We call
a_{M,N}(z)
the
universal coefficient
of
M
and
N
and
R^{\mathrm{norm}}_{M,N_{z}}
the
normalized
R
-matrix
.

Let
d_{M,N}(z)\in\mathbf{k}[z]
be a monic polynomial of the smallest degree such that the image of
d_{M,N}(z)R^{\mathrm{norm}}_{M,N_{z}}
is contained in
N_{z}\otimes M
. We call
d_{M,N}(z)
the
denominator
of
R^{\mathrm{norm}}_{M,N}
. Then we have

(2.6)

R^{\mathrm{ren}}_{M,N_{z}}\mathbin{:=}d_{M,N}(z)R^{\mathrm{norm}}_{M,N}\colon M\otimes N_{z}\to N_{z}\otimes M\quad\text{up to a constant multiple of $\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]$},

and the
R
-matrix

{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{M,N}}$}}\colon M\mathop{\otimes}N\to N\mathop{\otimes}M

is equal to the specialization of
R^{\mathrm{ren}}_{M,N_{z}}
at
z=1
up to a constant multiple. Thus

R^{\mathrm{ren}}_{M,N_{z}}=\dfrac{d_{M,N}(z)}{a_{M,N}(z)}R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}\quad\text{{and}}\quad c_{M,N}(z)=\dfrac{d_{M,N}(z)}{a_{M,N}(z)}.

The following theorem tells that the denominators control the representation theory of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
:

Theorem 2.1

(
[
AK97
,
Cha02
,
Kas02
,
KKKO15a
]
; see also
[
KKK15
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

(a)

For good modules
M,N
, the zeros of
d_{M,N}(z)
belong to

\mathbb{C}[[q^{1/m}]]q^{1/m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

(b)

For simple modules
M
and
N
such that one of them is real,
M_{x}
and
N_{y}
strongly commute to each other if and only if
d_{M,N}(z)d_{N,M}(z^{-1})
does not vanish at
z=x/y
.

(c)

Let
M_{k}
be a good module
with a dominant extremal vector
u_{k}
of weight
\lambda_{k}
and

a_{k}\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
for
k=1,\ldots,t
.
Assume that
a_{j}/a_{i}
is not a zero of
d_{M_{i},M_{j}}(z)
for any

1\leq i<j\leq t
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

(M_{1})_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(M_{t})_{a_{t}}
is generated by
u_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{t}
.

(ii)

The head of
(M_{1})_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(M_{t})_{a_{t}}
is simple.

(iii)

Any non-zero submodule of
(M_{t})_{a_{t}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(M_{1})_{a_{1}}
contains the vector
u_{t}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{1}
.

(iv)

The socle of
(M_{t})_{a_{t}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(M_{1})_{a_{1}}
is simple.

(v)

{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{}}$}}\colon(M_{1})_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(M_{t})_{a_{t}}\to(M_{t})_{a_{t}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(M_{1})_{a_{1}}
be the specialization of
R^{{\rm norm}}_{M_{1},\ldots,M_{t}}\mathbin{:=}\prod\limits_{1\leq j<k\leq t}R^{{\rm norm}}_{M_{j},\,M_{k}}
at
z_{k}=a_{k}
.
Then the image of
{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{}}$}}
is simple and it coincides with the head of
(M_{1})_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(M_{t})_{a_{t}}

and also with the socle of
(M_{t})_{a_{t}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(M_{1})_{a_{1}}
.

(d)

For a simple integrable
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-module
M
, there exists
a finite sequence

\displaystyle\big((i_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(i_{t},a_{t})\big)\in I\times\mathbf{k}^{\times}

such that

d_{V(\varpi_{i_{k}}),V(\varpi_{i_{k^{\prime}}})}(a_{k^{\prime}}/a_{k})\mathbin{:=}d_{i_{k},i_{k^{\prime}}}(a_{k^{\prime}}/a_{k})\not=0
for
1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq t
,
and
M
is isomorphic to the head of
V(\varpi_{i_{1}})_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V(\varpi_{i_{t}})_{a_{t}}
.
In particular,
M
has the dominant extremal weight
\sum_{k=1}^{t}\varpi_{i_{k}}
.

For simple modules
M
and
N
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, let
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N
and
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\Delta$}}N
denote the head and the socle of
M\mathop{\otimes}N
, respectively.

Proposition 2.2

(
[
KKKO15a
, Theorem 3.12]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be simple modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and assume that one of them is real.

(i)

\operatorname{Hom}(M\mathop{\otimes}N,N\mathop{\otimes}M)=\mathbf{k}\hskip 0.86108pt{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{M,N}}$}}.

(ii)

M\mathop{\otimes}N
and
N\mathop{\otimes}M
have simple socles and simple heads.
Moreover,
\operatorname{Im}({\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{M,N}}$}})
is isomorphic to
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N
and
N\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\Delta$}}M
.

(iii)

M\mathop{\otimes}N
is simple whenever its head and its socle are isomorphic to each other.

Proposition 2.3

(
[
KKKO15a
, Corollary 3.11]
)
.

(i)

Let
M_{k}

(k\in\{1,2,3\})
be modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, let
\varphi_{1}\colon L\to M_{2}\mathop{\otimes}M_{3}
and
\varphi_{2}\colon M_{1}\otimes M_{2}\to L^{\prime}
be non-zero homomorphism. Assume further
M_{2}
is a simple module. Then the composition

\textstyle{M_{1}\otimes L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M_{1}\otimes\varphi_{1}\ \ \hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{M_{1}\mathop{\otimes}M_{2}\mathop{\otimes}M_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \hskip 8.19447pt\varphi_{2}\otimes M_{3}}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\mathop{\otimes}M_{3}}
does not vanish.

(ii)

Let
M_{k}

(k\in\{1,2,3\})
be modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, let
\varphi_{1}\colon L\to M_{1}\mathop{\otimes}M_{2}
and
\varphi_{2}\colon M_{2}\otimes M_{3}\to L^{\prime}
be non-zero homomorphism. Assume further
M_{2}
is a simple module. The the composition

\textstyle{L\otimes M_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M_{1}\otimes\varphi_{1}\ \ \hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{M_{1}\mathop{\otimes}M_{2}\mathop{\otimes}M_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \hskip 8.19447pt\varphi_{2}\otimes M_{3}}
\textstyle{M_{1}\mathop{\otimes}L^{\prime}}
does not vanish.

We call the properties in Proposition
2.3

quasi-rigidity
(see
[
KKOP24a
, §6.2]
for more detail).

Lemma 2.4

(
[
KKKO15a
, Corollary 3.13]
)
.

Let
L
be a real simple module. Then for any simple module
X
, we have

\displaystyle(L\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}X)\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}\mathscr{D}L\simeq X,\qquad\mathscr{D}^{-1}L\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}(X\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L)\simeq X,

\displaystyle L\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}(X\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}\mathscr{D}L)\simeq X,\qquad(\mathscr{D}^{-1}L\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}X)\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L\simeq X.

2.2.
\mathbb{Z}
-invariants arising from the
R
-matrix

In this subsection, we review the
\mathbb{Z}
-invariants arising from
R
-matrices of quantum affine algebras, which are introduced in
[
KKOP20
]
.

We set

\widetilde{p}\mathbin{:=}p^{*2}=q^{2\langle c,\uprho\rangle}\text{ and }\varphi(z)\mathbin{:=}\prod_{s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}(1-\widetilde{p}^{s}z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^{n}\widetilde{p}^{n(n-1)/2}}{\prod_{k=1}^{n}(1-\widetilde{p}^{k})}z^{n}\in\mathbf{k}[\hskip-0.86108pt[z]\hskip-0.86108pt].

We define the multiplicative subgroup
\mathcal{G}
in
\mathbf{k}(\hskip-1.72218pt(z)\hskip-1.72218pt)^{\times}
containing
\mathbf{k}(z)^{\times}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\mathbin{:=}\left\{cz^{m}\prod_{a\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}}\varphi(az)^{\eta_{a}}\left|\begin{matrix}c\in\mathbf{k}^{\times},\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\ \eta_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ vanishes except finitely many $a$'s}\end{matrix}\right.\right\}.

Then it is proved in
[
KKOP20
]
that
c_{M,N}(z)
is contained in
\mathcal{G}
for rationally renormalizable
R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}
.

In
[
KKOP20
, Section 3]
, the following group homomorphisms
are introduced

\operatorname{Deg}\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{{and}}\quad\operatorname{Deg}^{\infty}\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{Z}

defined by

\operatorname{Deg}(f(z))\mathbin{:=}\sum_{a\in\widetilde{p}^{\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}}}\eta_{a}-\sum_{a\in\widetilde{p}^{\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}}\eta_{a}\quad\text{{and}}\quad\operatorname{Deg}^{\infty}(f(z))\mathbin{:=}\sum_{a\in\widetilde{p}^{\mathbb{Z}}}\eta_{a}

for
f(z)=cz_{m}\prod\varphi(az)^{\eta_{a}}\in\mathcal{G}
. Here
\widetilde{p}^{S}\mathbin{:=}\{\widetilde{p}^{k}\ |\ k\in S\}
for a subset
S
of
\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 2.5

(
[
KKOP20
, Definition 3.6, 3.14]
)
.

Let
M,N\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(1)

If
R^{\mathrm{univ}}_{M,N_{z}}
is rationally renormalizable, we define the integers
\Lambda(M,N)
and
\Lambda^{\infty}(M,N)
by

\Lambda(M,N)=\operatorname{Deg}(c_{M,N}(z))\quad\text{{and}}\quad\Lambda^{\infty}(M,N)=\operatorname{Deg}^{\infty}(c_{M,N}(z)).

(2)

For simple modules
M
and
N
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we define the integer
\mathfrak{d}(M,N)
by

\mathfrak{d}(M,N)=\dfrac{1}{2}\big(\Lambda(M,N)+\Lambda(\mathscr{D}^{-1}M,N)\big).

Proposition 2.6

(
[
KKOP20
, Proposition 3.16]
)
.

For simple modules
M
and
N
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we have

\mathfrak{d}(M,N)=\operatorname{zero}_{z=1}\big(d_{M,N}(z)d_{N,M}(z^{-1})\big),

where
\operatorname{zero}_{z=a}f(z)
denotes the order of zero of
f(z)
at
z=a
. In particular
\mathfrak{d}(M,N)\geq 0

Proposition 2.7

(
[
KKOP20
, Corollary 3.17]
)
.

For simple modules
M,N\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, assume that one of them is real.

(a)

M\otimes N
has a simple head and a simple socle.

(b)

\mathfrak{d}(M,N)=0
, then
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N\simeq M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\Delta$}}N
and hence
M\mathop{\otimes}N
is simple.

Proposition 2.8

(
[
KO18
, Lemma 7.3]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be simple modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Assume that one of them is real and
\mathfrak{d}(M,N)=1
.
Then we have an exact sequence

0\to M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\Delta$}}N\to M\mathop{\otimes}N\to M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N\to 0.

In particular,
M\mathop{\otimes}N
has composition length
2
.

Proposition 2.9

(
[
KKOP20
, Corollary 4.2]
)
.

For simple modules
L,M,N\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we have

\mathfrak{d}(S,L)\leq\mathfrak{d}(M,L)+\mathfrak{d}(N,L)

for any simple subquotient
S
of
M\otimes N
.

Proposition 2.10

(
[
KKOP24c
, Lemma 2.28]
)
.

For real simple modules
M
and
N
, if
\mathfrak{d}(M,N)\leq 1
, then
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N
is real.

Proposition 2.11

(
[
KKOP20
,
KKOP24c
]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be simple modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then we have the following:

(i)

\mathfrak{d}(M,N)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathfrak{d}(M,N)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\Lambda(M,N)+\Lambda(N,M)\big)=\mathfrak{d}(N,M)
.

(ii)

\Lambda(M,N)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k+\delta(k<0)}\mathfrak{d}(M,\mathscr{D}^{k}N)
.

(iii)

\Lambda(M,N)=\Lambda(\mathscr{D}^{-1}N,M)=\Lambda(N,\mathscr{D}M)
.

(iv)

\Lambda^{\infty}(M,N)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\mathfrak{d}(M,\mathscr{D}^{k}N)

Lemma 2.12

(
[
KKOP24c
, Corollary 2.25]
)
.

Let
L,M
be real simple modules and
X
a simple module.

(i)

If
\mathfrak{d}(L,M)=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}L,M)=0
, then we have
\mathfrak{d}(L,X\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}M)=\mathfrak{d}(L,X)
.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{d}(L,M)=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{-1}L,M)=0
, then we have
\mathfrak{d}(L,M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}X)=\mathfrak{d}(L,X)
.

Proposition 2.13

(
[
KKOP24b
, Proposition 2.25]
)
.

Let
M
,
N
and
L
be simple
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules such that
L
is
real. Assume that

(i)

\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}M,L)=0
,

(ii)

\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}L,N)=0
, and

(iii)

M\mathop{\otimes}L\mathop{\otimes}N
has a simple head.

Then we have

\mathfrak{d}(L,{\operatorname{hd}}(M\mathop{\otimes}L\mathop{\otimes}N))=\mathfrak{d}(L,M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L)+\mathfrak{d}(L,L\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N).

Lemma 2.14

(
[
KKOP21
, Corollary 3.18]
)
.

Let
L
be a real simple module and
M
be a module in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and assume that any simple subquotient
S
of
M
satisfies
\mathfrak{d}(L,S)\leq n
.
Then any simple subquotient
K
of
L\mathop{\otimes}M
satisfies
\mathfrak{d}(L,K)<n
. In particular, any simple subquotient of
L^{\mathop{\otimes}n}\mathop{\otimes}M

strongly commutes with
L
.

2.3.
Normal sequences and root modules

A sequence
\underline{L}
of simple modules is called a
normal sequence
if the composition of
R
-matrices

\displaystyle{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{L_{r},\ldots,L_{1}}}$}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<k\leq r}{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{L_{k},L_{i}}}$}}=({\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{L_{2},L_{1}}}$}})\circ\cdots\circ({\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{L_{r-1},L_{1}}}$}}\circ\cdots\circ{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{L_{r-1},L_{r-2}}}$}})\circ({\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{L_{r},L_{1}}}$}}\circ\cdots\circ{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{L_{r},L_{r-1}}}$}})

\displaystyle\colon L_{r}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}L_{1}\to L_{1}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}L_{r}\text{ does not vanish}.

An ordered sequence of simple modules
\underline{L}=(L_{r},L_{r-1}\ldots,L_{1})
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is called
almost real
, if all
L_{i}

(1\leq i\leq r)
are real except for at most one.

Lemma 2.15

(
[
KK19
,
KKOP23
]
)
.

Let
\underline{L}=(L_{r},\ldots,L_{1})
be an almost real sequence. If
\underline{L}
is normal, then

(2.7)

the image of
{\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{\underline{L}}}$}}
is simple and coincides with the head of
L_{r}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}L_{1}

and also with the socle of
L_{1}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}L_{r}
.

Moreover,
\underline{L}
is normal if and only if either

(i)

\underline{L}^{\prime}=(L_{r-1},\ldots,L_{1})
is a normal sequence and
\Lambda\big(L_{r},\operatorname{Im}({\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{\underline{L}^{\prime}}}$}})\big)=\sum_{k=1}^{r-1}\Lambda(L_{r},L_{k})
, or

(ii)

\underline{L}^{\prime\prime}=(L_{r},\ldots,L_{2})
is a normal sequence and
\Lambda\big(\operatorname{Im}({\mathbf{r}}_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{\underline{L}^{\prime\prime}}}$}}),L_{1}\big)=\sum_{k=2}^{r}\Lambda(L_{k},L_{1})
.

Proposition 2.16

(
[
KKOP25b
, Proposition 1.10]
)
.

Let
\underline{L}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{r})
be an almost real normal sequence.

(i)

Any simple subquotient
S
of
L_{2}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}L_{r}
satisfies
\Lambda(L_{1},S)\leq\sum_{k=2}^{r}\Lambda(L_{1},L_{k})
.

(ii)

Any simple subquotient
S
of
L_{1}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}L_{r-1}
satisfies
\Lambda(S,L_{r})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r-1}\Lambda(L_{k},L_{r})
.

(iii)

{\operatorname{hd}}(L_{1}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}L_{r})
appears only once in the composition series of
L_{1}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}L_{r}
.

Lemma 2.17

(
[
KKOP20
, Lemma 4.3, Lemma 4.17]
;
[
KKOP24c
, Lemma 2.24]
)
.

Let
L,M,N
be simple modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
that are all real except for at most one.

(a)

Assume that one of the following condition holds:

(i)

\mathfrak{d}(L,M)=0
and
L
is real,

(ii)

\mathfrak{d}(M,N)=0
and
N
is real,

(iii)

\mathfrak{d}(L,\mathscr{D}^{-1}N)=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}L,N)=0
and
L
or
N
is real,

then
(L,M,N)
is a normal sequence.

(b)

Assume that
L
is real.

(iv)

(L,M,N)
is normal if and only if
(M,N,\mathscr{D}L)
is normal.

(v)

\mathfrak{d}(L,M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N)=\mathfrak{d}(L,M)+\mathfrak{d}(L,N)
if and only if
(L,M,N)
and
(M,N,L)
are normal.

Definition 2.18

(
[
KKOP24c
,
KKOP24b
]
)
.

Let
(M,N)
be an ordered pair of simple modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(1)

We call the pair
unmixed
if

\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}M,N)=0

and
strongly unmixed
if

\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{k}M,N)=0\quad\text{for any }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

(2)

An almost real sequence
\underline{M}=(M_{s},M_{s-1},\ldots,M_{1})
is said to be
(
strongly
)
unmixed
if
(M_{k},M_{i})
is (strongly) unmixed for all
s\geq k>i\geq 1
.

Proposition 2.19

(
[
KKOP24c
]
)
.

(a)

Any unmixed almost real sequence
\underline{M}=(M_{s},M_{s-1},\ldots,M_{1})
is normal.

(b)

For a strongly unmixed almost real sequence
\underline{M}=(M_{s},M_{s-1},\ldots,M_{1})
,

\bigl(\operatorname{hd}(M_{s}\mathop{\otimes}M_{s-1}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots M_{k}),\operatorname{hd}(M_{k-1}\mathop{\otimes}M_{k-1}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots M_{1})\bigr)

is strongly unmixed for any
1<k\leq r
.

We say a real simple module
L
a
root module
if

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(L,\mathscr{D}^{k}(L))=\delta(k=\pm 1)\quad\text{ for any }k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Proposition 2.20

(
[
KKOP24b
, Proposition 2.28]
)
.

Every fundamental module is a root module.

For the rest of this subsection, we take root modules
L,L^{\prime}
satisfying

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{k}L,L^{\prime})=\delta(k=0)\quad\text{ for }k\in\mathbb{Z}.

We call a pair of root modules satisfying (
2.9
) a
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
-pair
.

Lemma 2.21

(
[
KKOP24c
, Lemma 3.8, Lemma 3.9]
)
.

We have

\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{k}L,L\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L^{\prime})=\delta(k=1)\quad\text{{and}}\quad\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{k}L,L^{\prime}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L)=\delta(k=-1).

Furthermore, the simple module
L\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L^{\prime}
is a root module.

Lemma 2.22

(
[
KKOP24c
, Lemma 3.10]
)
.

Let
X
be a simple module.

(i)

If
k\neq 0,1
, then

\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{k}L,L^{\prime}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}X)=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{k}L,X).

As for
k=0
and
1
, one and only one of the following two statements is true.

(a)

\mathfrak{d}(L,L^{\prime}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}X)=\mathfrak{d}(L,X)
and
\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}L,L^{\prime}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}X)=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}L,X)-1
,

(b)

\mathfrak{d}(L,L^{\prime}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}X)=\mathfrak{d}(L,X)+1
and
\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}L,L^{\prime}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}X)=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}L,X)
.

(ii)

If
k\neq-1,0
, then

\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{k}L,X\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L^{\prime})=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{k}L,X).

As for
k=-1
and
0
, one and only one of the following two
statements is true.

(c)

\mathfrak{d}(L,X\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L^{\prime})=\mathfrak{d}(L,X)
and
\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{-1}L,X\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L^{\prime})=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{-1}L,X)-1
,

(d)

\mathfrak{d}(L,X\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L^{\prime})=\mathfrak{d}(L,X)+1
and
\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{-1}L,X\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L^{\prime})=\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{-1}L,X)
.

3.
KR modules,
i
-box combinatorics and Dorey’s rule

In this section, we first recall the KR modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
and interpret them in terms of
i
-boxes, recently introduced in
[
KKOP24b
]
.
Then, using
i
-boxes, we investigate strongly commuting conditions of two KR modules in terms of their (extended) reaches.
In the final subsection, we recall the generalized Dorey’s rule among the fundamental modules.

3.1.
Kirillov–Reshetikhin modules

Note that the simple objects in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
are parameterized by
I_{0}
-tuples of polynomials
\mathcal{P}=(\mathcal{P}_{i}(u))_{i\in I_{0}}
,
where
\mathcal{P}_{i}(u)\in\mathbf{k}[u]
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}(0)=1
for
i\in I_{0}

[
CP95a
,
CP98
]
.
We denote by
(\mathcal{P}^{V}_{i}(u))_{i\in I_{0}}
the polynomials corresponding to a simple module
V
, and call
(\mathcal{P}^{V}_{i}(u))_{i\in I_{0}}
the
Drinfel’d polynomials
of
V
. The Drinfel’d polynomials
(\mathcal{P}^{V}_{i}(u))_{i\in I_{0}}
are determined by the eigenvalues
of the simultaneously commuting actions of some
Drinfel’d generators
of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
on a subspace of
V
(see, e.g.,
[
CP95a
]
for more details).

A
Kirillov–Reshetikhin module
(KR module), denoted by
W^{(k)}_{m,a}
for
k\in I_{0}
,
m\geq 1
, and
a\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
, is a simple module of dominant extremal weight
m\varpi_{k}
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
whose Drinfel’d polynomials
\mathcal{P}=(\mathcal{P}_{i},\mid i\in I_{0})
are

\mathcal{P}_{i}(u)=\delta_{i,k}(1-au)(1-aq_{k}^{2}u)(1-aq_{k}^{4}u)\cdots(1-aq_{k}^{2m-2}u)+(1-\delta_{i,k})\quad(i\in I_{0})

unless
k=n
and
\mathfrak{g}=A^{(2)}_{2n}
, in which case

\mathcal{P}_{i}(u)=\delta_{i,n}(1-au)(1-aq^{2}u)(1-aq^{4}u)\cdots(1-aq^{2m-2}u)+\delta(i\neq n).

For any
a\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
, it is well-known that the sequence of fundamental modules

\boldsymbol{(}{V(\varpi_{k})_{a(-\check{q}_{k})^{2m}},\ldots,V(\varpi_{k})_{a(-\check{q}_{k})^{2}},V(\varpi_{k})_{a}}\boldsymbol{)}

is contained in the same skeleton category and unmixed. Hence it is a normal sequence for
m\geq 1
and
k\in I_{0}
.
Here
\check{q}_{k}\mathbin{:=}q_{k}
unless
\mathfrak{g}=A_{2n}^{(2)}
and
k=n
, in which case
\check{q}_{n}=q
.
We denote
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\mathbin{:=}W^{(k)}_{m,(-\check{q}_{k})^{1-m}}
for
k\in I_{0}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, which can be constructed as

\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\simeq\begin{cases}{\operatorname{hd}}\left(V(\varpi_{k})_{(-q_{k})^{m-1}}\mathop{\otimes}V(\varpi_{k})_{(-q_{k})^{m-3}}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}V(\varpi_{k})_{(-q_{k})^{1-m}}\right)&\text{ if $\mathfrak{g}$ is untwisted, }\\
{\operatorname{hd}}\left(V(\varpi_{k})_{(-q)^{m-1}}\mathop{\otimes}V(\varpi_{k})_{(-q)^{m-3}}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}V(\varpi_{k})_{(-q)^{1-m}}\right)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

(see also
[
Her10a
]
).
It is also well-known that
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a}
is a real simple prime module and
\mathscr{D}(\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a})\simeq\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{*m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a(p^{*})}
(see
[
Cha02
, (2.20)]
).

By Proposition
2.19
and the definition of
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)
, we have surjective homomorphisms as follows:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m-t}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{k})^{t}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{t}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{k})^{t-m}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big),

for
k\in I_{0}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
and
m>t\geq 1
.
We call the homomorphism (
3.1
) a
fusion rule
.

The branching rule of
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a}
to
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
-modules is known
[
Cha01
,
Her06
,
Her10a
,
Nak03
]
(see also, e.g.,
[
Oka13
,
Scr20
]
).
Let us describe some particular cases.
We call a node
k\in I_{0}

special
if there exists an automorphism
\psi
of the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
k=\psi(0)
, in which case
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\simeq V(m\Lambda_{k})
(as
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
-modules)
[
KKM+92b
, Sec. 1]
.
When
\mathfrak{g}
is dual to an untwisted affine type (including all simply-laced types), then we additionally call such
k

minuscule
and can be classified by
\langle\alpha^{\vee},\Lambda_{i}\rangle\leq 2
for all positive roots
\alpha
of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, where
\alpha^{\vee}
denotes the corresponding dual root.
When
k
is the adjoint node (when
\mathfrak{g}
is not type
A_{n}^{(1)}
), then
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\simeq\bigoplus_{s=0}^{m}V(s\Lambda_{k})

[
KKM+92b
, Sec. 1]
.
If
\langle\alpha^{\vee},\Lambda_{i}\rangle\leq 2
for all positive roots
\alpha
, then this decomposition is multiplicity free, which is true for all
k\in I_{0}
in nonexceptional affine types.

Note that KR module
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a}

(a\in\mathbf{k}^{\times})
with
m=1
is a fundamental module.
For
k,l\in I_{0}
, the universal coefficient
a_{l,k}(z)\mathbin{:=}a_{V(\varpi_{l}),V(\varpi_{k})}(z)
in (
2.5
)
is described as follows (see
[
AK97
, Appendix A]
):

(3.2)

\displaystyle a_{l,k}(z)\equiv\dfrac{\prod_{\nu}(p^{*}y_{\nu}z;q)_{\infty}(p^{*}\overline{y_{\nu}}z;q)_{\infty}}{\prod_{\nu}(x_{\nu}z;q)_{\infty}(p^{*2}\overline{x_{\nu}}z;q)_{\infty}}\quad\pmod{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]^{\times}},

where
(y;x)_{\infty}=\prod_{s=0}^{\infty}(1-y^{s}x)
,

d_{l,k}(z)=\prod_{\nu}(z-x_{\nu})\quad\text{ and }\quad d_{l^{*},k}(z)=\prod_{\nu}(z-y_{\nu}).

Here and after, for
f(z),g(z)\in\mathbf{k}(\!(z)\!)
, we write

f(z)\equiv g(z)\quad\text{if }f(z)/g(z)\in\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]^{\times}.

Note that the denominators of the normalized
R
-matrices
d_{l,k}(z)
, and hence the universal coefficients
a_{l,k}(z)
,
were calculated in
[
AK97
,
DO94
,
KKK15
,
Oh15
]
for classical affine types and in
[
OS19a
,
OS19b
]
for exceptional affine types.

Equation (
3.2
) can be generalized to KR modules for
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l^{p}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)
and
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)
(
m,p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
) by following the same argument in
[
AK97
, Appendix A]
:

(3.3)

\displaystyle a_{l^{p},k^{m}}(z)\mathbin{:=}a_{\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.60275pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l^{p}}}\hskip 0.60275pt\big)),\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.60275pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.60275pt\big)}(z)\equiv\dfrac{\prod_{\nu}(p^{*}y_{\nu}z;q)_{\infty}(p^{*}\overline{y_{\nu}}z;q)_{\infty}}{\prod_{\nu}(x_{\nu}z;q)_{\infty}(p^{*2}\overline{x_{\nu}}z;q)_{\infty}}\quad\pmod{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]^{\times}},

where

d_{l^{p},k^{m}}(z)\mathbin{:=}d_{\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.60275pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l^{p}}}\hskip 0.60275pt\big),\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.60275pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.60275pt\big)}(z)=\prod_{\nu}(z-x_{\nu}),\quad d_{(l^{*})^{p},k^{m}}(z)\mathbin{:=}d_{\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.60275pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{(l^{*})^{p}}}\hskip 0.60275pt\big),\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.60275pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.60275pt\big)}(z)=\prod_{\nu}(z-y_{\nu}).

The following lemmas are easily checked.

Lemma 3.1

(cf.
[
Cha02
]
)
.

For
l,k\in I_{0}
, assume that the zeros of
d_{l^{p},k^{m}}(z)
belong to
\mathbb{C}[[q^{1/m}]]q^{1/m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then we have

d_{l^{p},k^{m}}(z)\equiv d_{k^{m},l^{p}}(z)\pmod{\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]^{\times}}.

Remark 3.2
.

As a consequence of the result of this paper,
we can conclude that
d_{l^{p},k^{m}}(z)
belong to
\mathbb{C}[[q^{1/m}]]q^{1/m}
for all classical affine types
(see Section
6.2
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
k^{\prime}

(
resp.
l^{\prime})
be in the orbit of
k

(
resp.
l)
under some
(
classical type
)
Dynkin diagram automorphism.
Then

d_{l^{p},k^{m}}(z)=d_{(l^{\prime})^{p},(k^{\prime})^{m}}(z).

The following proposition is one of the main ingredients of this paper.

Proposition 3.4

(
[
AK97
, Lemma C.15]
)
.

Let
M,M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}
and
N
be simple
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules. Assume that we have a surjective
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-homomorphism

M^{\prime}\otimes M^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow M
.
Then we have

(3.4a)

\displaystyle\dfrac{d_{N,M^{\prime}}(z)d_{N,M^{\prime\prime}}(z)a_{N,M}(z)}{d_{N,M}(z)a_{N,M^{\prime}}(z)a_{N,M^{\prime\prime}}(z)}\in\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}]

and

(3.4b)

\displaystyle\dfrac{d_{M^{\prime},N}(z)d_{M^{\prime\prime},N}(z)a_{M,N}(z)}{d_{M,N}(z)a_{M^{\prime},N}(z)a_{M^{\prime\prime},N}(z)}\in\mathbf{k}[z^{\pm 1}].

3.2.
i
-boxes and T-systems

Recall that for each quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, we fix
{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{fin}}}
and
{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}
.
Also we can choose a compatible reading

\mathfrak{R}=(\ldots,(\imath_{-1},p_{-1}),(\imath_{0},p_{0}),(\imath_{1},p_{1}),\ldots)\text{ of }{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}.

Throughout this subsection, we fix a compatible reading
\mathfrak{R}
and recall Convention
Convention
.
Thus we skip
R
or
R
in notations if there is no danger of confusion.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the fundamental module

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}[k]\mathbin{:=}\mathcal{V}(\imath_{k},p_{k}),\quad\text{where $\uppsi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{${\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{R}}}$}}(k)=(\imath_{k},p_{k})$}.

Proposition 3.5

(
[
KKOP24c
, Proposition 5.7]
)
.

For any
a<b
, the ordered pair
(\mathfrak{R}[b],\mathfrak{R}[a])
is strongly unmixed.

For
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\jmath\in\blacktriangle_{0}
, we define

(3.6)

\displaystyle s_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{${\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{R}}}$}}^{+}

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\min\{t\mid s<t,\;\imath_{t}=\imath_{s}\},

\displaystyle s_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{${\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{R}}}$}}^{-}

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\max\{t\mid t<s,\;\imath_{t}=\imath_{s}\},

\displaystyle s_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{${\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{R}}}$}}(\jmath)^{+}

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\min\{t\mid s\leq t,\;\imath_{t}=\jmath\},

\displaystyle s_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{${\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{R}}}$}}(\jmath)^{-}

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\max\{t\mid t\leq s,\;\imath_{t}=\jmath\}.

Lemma 3.6

(
[
KKOP25a
, §6.1]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{R}
be a compatible reading of
{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}
.

(i)

p_{s^{+}}=p_{s}+2d_{{\overline{\imath}}}
.

(ii)

p_{t}=p_{s}+\min(d_{\overline{\imath_{s}}},d_{\overline{\imath_{t}}})
if
d_{\blacktriangle}(\imath_{s},\imath_{t})=1
and
t^{-}<s<t<s^{+}
.

(iii)

p_{t}-(p_{s}+2)\in 2d_{\overline{\imath_{s}}}\mathbb{Z}
if
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
\imath_{t}=\sigma(\imath_{s})
.

(iv)

Let
\imath_{s},\imath_{t}\in\blacktriangle_{0}
with
d_{\blacktriangle}(\imath_{s},\imath_{t})=1
. Then

(a)

p_{s}<p_{t}
if and only if
s<t
.

(b)

p_{s}-p_{t}-\min(d_{\overline{\imath_{s}}},d_{\overline{\imath_{t}}})\in 2\min(d_{\overline{\imath_{s}}},d_{\overline{\imath_{t}}})\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 3.7

(
[
KKOP24b
, Deﬁnition 4.14]
)
.

(1)

We say that an interval
[a,b]
is an
i
-box associated with
\mathfrak{R}
if

-\infty<a\leq b<+\infty
and
\imath_{a}=\imath_{b}
.
We sometimes write it as
[a,b]^{\mathfrak{R}}
to emphasize that it is associated with
\mathfrak{R}
.

(2)

For an
i
-box
[a,b]
, we set

|[a,b]|_{\phi}\mathbin{:=}\big|\{s\mid\text{$s\in[a,b]$
and $\imath_{a}=\imath_{s}=\imath_{b}$}\}\big|.

(3)

For an
i
-box
[a,b]
, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}[a,b]\mathbin{:=}{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathfrak{R}[b]\mathop{\otimes}\mathfrak{R}[b^{-}]\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}\mathfrak{R}[a^{+}]\mathop{\otimes}\mathfrak{R}[a]).

By Proposition
3.5
and Lemma
3.6
, the sequence of root modules
(\mathfrak{R}[b],\mathfrak{R}[b^{-}],\dotsc,\mathfrak{R}[a^{+}],\mathfrak{R}[a])
is normal and
\mathfrak{R}[a,b]
is a KR module.
Note that
\mathrm{clr}(\mathfrak{R}[a,b])
of
\mathfrak{R}[a,b]
is canonically defined as
\imath_{a}=\imath_{b}
.

In order to compute the
\mathfrak{d}
-invariants between KR modules in the sequel, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
[a,b]
be an
i
-box associated with
\mathfrak{R}
.

(i)

For a KR module
\mathfrak{R}[a,b]
, we call an interval

\mathrm{rch}(\mathfrak{R}[a,b])\mathbin{:=}\boldsymbol{[}{p_{a},p_{b}}\boldsymbol{]}

the
reach
of
\mathfrak{R}[a,b]
.
Here we use bold bracket
\boldsymbol{[}{\ ,\ }\boldsymbol{]}
to distinguish it with an
i
-box
[\ ,\ ]
associated with
\mathfrak{R}

and to emphasize it as a reach in
{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}
.

(ii)

For
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\imath\in\blacktriangle_{0}
, we set

p_{\leq}(\imath)\mathbin{:=}\max\{r\ |\ r\leq p,\ (\imath,r)\in{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}_{0})\quad\text{{and}}\quad p_{\geq}(\imath)\mathbin{:=}\min\{r\ |\ r\geq p,\ (\imath,r)\in{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}_{0}).

In particular, note that
s_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{${\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{R}}}$}}(\imath)^{+}
(resp.
s_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{${\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{R}}}$}}(\imath)^{-}
) in (
3.6
) is different from
p_{\geq}(\imath)
(resp.
p_{\leq}(\imath)
) as the former depends on
\mathfrak{R}
while the latter do not.

(iii)

For a KR module
\mathfrak{R}[a,b]
with its reach
\boldsymbol{[}{p_{a},p_{b}}\boldsymbol{]}
and
\imath_{a}=\imath
, and
\jmath\in\blacktriangle_{0}
, we call an interval

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{exrch}_{\jmath}(\mathfrak{R}[a,b])\mathbin{:=}\boldsymbol{[}{(\widetilde{p}_{a})_{\leq}(\jmath)-2d_{{\overline{\jmath}}},\;(\widetilde{p}_{b})_{\geq}(\jmath)+2d_{{\overline{\jmath}}}}\boldsymbol{]}

the
\jmath
-extended reach
of
\mathfrak{R}[a,b]
, where
\widetilde{p}_{a}\mathbin{:=}p_{a}-\widetilde{d}_{\blacktriangle,\sigma}(\imath,\jmath)

and
\widetilde{p}_{b}\mathbin{:=}p_{b}+\widetilde{d}_{\blacktriangle,\sigma}(\imath,\jmath)
.

Note that, for a given KR module
M
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
, its reach and extended reaches do not depend on the choice of
a compatible reading
\mathfrak{R}
.

Example 3.9
.

Let us consider the repetition quiver
{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}
of type
B_{3}^{(1)}
given in Example
1.4
(
ii
).

(i)

For
p=-4\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\imath=1\in\blacktriangle_{0}
, we have
p_{\leq}(\imath)=-6
and
p_{\geq}(\imath)=-2
.

(ii)

Let us consider the compatible reading
\mathfrak{R}
in Example
1.5
(
b
) with
(\imath_{1},p_{1})=(2,0)
.
Then we have
\mathrm{rch}(\mathfrak{R}[0,3])=\boldsymbol{[}{-1,1}\boldsymbol{]}
,
\mathrm{exrch}_{3}(\mathfrak{R}[0,3])=\boldsymbol{[}{-3,3}\boldsymbol{]}

\mathrm{exrch}_{1}(\mathfrak{R}[-2,4])=\boldsymbol{[}{-13,10}\boldsymbol{]}
and
\mathrm{exrch}_{5}(\mathfrak{R}[-1,5])=\boldsymbol{[}{-8,8}\boldsymbol{]}
.

Proposition 3.10

(
[
KKOP24b
]
)
.

Let
[a,b]
an
i
-box associated with a compatible reading
\mathfrak{R}
.

(i)

For
s\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
a^{-}<s<b^{+}
, we have
\mathfrak{d}(\mathfrak{R}[a,b],\mathfrak{R}[s])=0
.

(ii)

We have
\mathfrak{d}(\mathfrak{R}[b^{+}],\mathfrak{R}[a,b])=\mathfrak{d}(\mathfrak{R}[a^{-}],\mathfrak{R}[a,b])=1
.

(iii)

We have
\mathfrak{d}(\mathfrak{R}[a,b],\mathfrak{R}[a^{-},b^{-}])=1.

(iv)

We have
\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}\mathfrak{R}[b],\mathfrak{R}[a,b])=1
and
\mathfrak{d}(\mathscr{D}^{-1}\mathfrak{R}[a],\mathfrak{R}[a,b])=1.

Definition 3.11
.

We say that
i
-boxes
[a_{1},b_{1}]
and
[a_{2},b_{2}]

commute
if
we have either

a_{1}^{-}<a_{2}\leq b_{2}<b_{1}^{+}\quad\text{ or }\quad a_{2}^{-}<a_{1}\leq b_{1}<b_{2}^{+}.

Theorem 3.12

(
[
KKOP24b
]
)
.

For a compatible reading
\mathfrak{R}
, if two
i
-boxes
[a_{1},b_{1}]
and
[a_{2},b_{2}]
commute, then

\mathfrak{R}[a_{1},b_{1}]
and
\mathfrak{R}[a_{2},b_{2}]
commute.

Recall the two extremal compatible readings in Example
1.5
(
a
) and (
b
).
Then, using Lemma
3.6
and terms of reaches, Theorem
3.12
can be re-expressed as follows:
For KR modules

W_{1}
and
W_{2}
strongly commute
if either

(3.8)

\displaystyle{\rm(i)}\ \ p^{\prime}_{a_{2}}<p_{a_{1}}\leq p_{b_{1}}<p^{\prime}_{b_{2}}\quad\text{ or }\quad{\rm(ii)}\ \ p^{\prime}_{a_{1}}<p_{a_{2}}\leq p_{b_{2}}<p^{\prime}_{b_{1}},

where

W_{u}=\mathfrak{R}[a_{u},b_{u}]\text{ with }\imath_{a_{u}}=\imath_{b_{u}}=\imath_{u},\ \ \mathrm{rch}(W_{u})=\boldsymbol{[}{p_{a_{u}},p_{b_{u}}}\boldsymbol{]},\ \ \text{ and }\mathrm{exrch}_{\imath_{v}}(W_{u})=\boldsymbol{[}{p^{\prime}_{a_{u}},p^{\prime}_{b_{u}}}\boldsymbol{]}

for
\{u,v\}=\{1,2\}
and some compatible reading
\mathfrak{R}
.

Theorem 3.13

(
[
Nak04
,
Her06
]
; see also
[
KKOP24b
, §4.6]
)
.

For an arbitrary quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, a compatible reading
\mathfrak{R}
of
{\widehat{\blacktriangle}}^{\sigma}
and an arbitrary

i
-box
[a,b]^{\mathfrak{R}}
such that
a<b
, we have a short exact sequence

(3.9)

\displaystyle 0\to\hskip-8.61108pt\mathop{\mbox{\scalebox{1.1}{\rule[-2.58334pt]{0.0pt}{4.30554pt}\normalsize$\bigotimes$}}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\jmath\in\blacktriangle_{0};\;d(\imath_{a},\jmath)=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.61108pt\mathfrak{R}[a(\jmath)^{+},b(\jmath)^{-}]\to\mathfrak{R}[a^{+},b]\mathop{\otimes}\mathfrak{R}[a,b^{-}]\to\mathfrak{R}[a,b]\mathop{\otimes}\mathfrak{R}[a^{+},b^{-}]\to 0,

where the left and right terms are real simple modules.

We call the exact sequence in (
3.9
) a
T-system
.
In Appendix
C
, we present the T-systems for all untwisted affine types.

3.3.
Generalized Dorey’s rule

In this subsection, we review the morphisms

(3.10)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}\big(V(\varpi_{i})_{a}\mathop{\otimes}V(\varpi_{j})_{b},V(\varpi_{k})_{c}\big)\quad\text{ for }i,j,k\in I_{0}\text{ and }a,b,c\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}

that are studied by
[
CP96b
,
FH15
,
KKK15
,
Oh15
,
Oh19
,
YZ11
]
.
The morphisms in (
3.10
) are called
Dorey’s rule
due to their origin from the work of Dorey
[
Dor91
,
Dor93
]
.

We keep the notation
{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{fin}}}
for a given
\mathfrak{g}^{(t)}_{n}
as defined in (
1.4
) and (
1.4
).

Proposition 3.14

(
[
BKM14
, Lemma 2.6]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{g}
be a finite simple Lie algebra.
For
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{\mathbf{g}}\setminus\Pi_{\mathbf{g}}
and any
{\widetilde{w}_{0}}
of
w_{0}\in W_{\mathbf{g}}
, a
[{\widetilde{w}_{0}}]
-minimal exponent of
\gamma
is indeed a pair
(\alpha,\beta)
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi^{+}_{\mathbf{g}}
such that
\alpha+\beta=\gamma
.

Recall the exponent
\underline{m}=\boldsymbol{\{}{\underline{m}_{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\}}_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\Phi^{+}}
in §
1.3
.
For a reduced expression
{\widetilde{w}_{0}}
of
w_{0}
adapted to a
\mathrm{Q}
-datum
\mathcal{Q}
, we set

\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\underline{m})\mathbin{:=}\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta^{\widetilde{w}_{0}}_{\ell})^{\mathop{\otimes}m_{\beta_{\ell}}}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta^{\widetilde{w}_{0}}_{2})^{\mathop{\otimes}m_{\beta_{2}}}\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta^{\widetilde{w}_{0}}_{1})^{\mathop{\otimes}m_{\beta_{1}}}.

Dorey’s rule for affine Kac-Moody algebra
\mathfrak{g}
can be described as follows.

Theorem 3.15

(
[
CP96b
,
Oh17
,
Oh16
,
Oh19
,
KO18
,
OS19b
]
)
.

For a quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, let
(i,x)
,
(j,y)
,
(k,z)\in I_{0}\times\mathbf{k}^{\times}
. Then

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}\big(V(\varpi_{j})_{y}\otimes V(\varpi_{i})_{x},V(\varpi_{k})_{z}\big)\neq 0

if and only if there exists a
\mathrm{Q}
-datum
\mathcal{Q}
of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\Phi_{{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{fin}}}}^{+}
such that

(i)

\{\alpha,\beta\}
is a pair of positive roots such that
\alpha\prec_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\beta
and
\alpha+\beta=\gamma
,

(ii)

V(\varpi_{j})_{y}=\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha)_{t},\ V(\varpi_{i})_{x}=\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta)_{t},\ V(\varpi_{k})_{z}=\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\gamma)_{t}
for some
t\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
.

Furthermore the followings holds:

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of affine type
A_{n}^{(t)}
,
D_{n}^{(t)}
or
E_{6,7,8}^{(t)}

(t=1,2,3)
, and
\mathcal{Q}
be its
\mathrm{Q}
-datum.
Assume that
\underline{p}=\{\alpha,\beta\}
is a non
[\mathcal{Q}]
-simple pair such that
\alpha\prec_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\beta
. Then there exits a unique
[\mathcal{Q}]
-simple exponent
\underline{s}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{u})

such that
\underline{s}={\operatorname{soc}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}(\underline{p})
and

{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha)\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta))\simeq{\operatorname{soc}}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta)\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha))\simeq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\underline{s})\text{ is simple.}

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of affine type
B_{n}^{(1)}
,
C_{n}^{(1)}
,
F_{4}^{(1)}
or
G_{2}^{(1)}
, and
\mathcal{Q}
be its
\mathrm{Q}
-datum.
Let
\underline{p}=(\alpha,\beta)
be a non
[\mathcal{Q}]
-simple pair such that
\alpha\prec_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\beta
, then the following holds:

(3.11)

(a)

if
\operatorname{dist}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}(\underline{p})=1
, then we have

{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha)\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta))\simeq{\operatorname{soc}}(\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta)\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha))\simeq\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Q}}({\operatorname{soc}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\{\alpha,\beta\}),

which is simple,

(b)

if
\operatorname{dist}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}(\underline{p})=2
and there exists a unique exponent
\underline{m}
satisfying

{\operatorname{soc}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}(\underline{p})\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\underline{m}\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\underline{p},\hskip 60.0pt

then we have
{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha)\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta))\simeq{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\underline{m}))
, which is a KR module.

Example 3.16
.

In (
4
), consider the pair
(\langle 1,-2\rangle,\langle 1,2\rangle)
. It is a
[\mathcal{Q}]
-minimal pair for the
[\mathcal{Q}]
-simple exponent
(\langle 1,-4\rangle,\langle 1,4\rangle)
.
Then Theorem
3.15
tells that we have a surjective homomorphism

\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,2\rangle)\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,-2\rangle)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,4\rangle)\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,-4\rangle),\text{ which is simple}.

Remark 3.17
.

By Proposition
2.7
,
\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha),\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta))>0
for a non
[\mathcal{Q}]
-simple pair
\underline{p}=(\alpha,\beta)
in Theorem
3.15
. Also, there are non
[\mathcal{Q}]
-simple pair

\underline{p}=(\alpha,\beta)
such that
\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha),\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta))>1
and
\underline{p}^{\prime}=(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
whose composition length of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\beta^{\prime})
is strictly larger than
2
. For instance,
consider the pair
(\langle 1,-3\rangle,\langle 2,3\rangle)
in (
4
). Then we have

\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,-3\rangle),\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 2,3\rangle)=2,\qquad\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,-3\rangle)\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 2,3\rangle)\simeq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,2\rangle)

and the composition length of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,-3\rangle)\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 2,3\rangle)

is strictly larger than
2

Remark 3.18
.

For
\mathfrak{g}=B_{n}^{(1)},C_{n}^{(1)},F_{4}^{(1)}
(resp.
G_{2}^{(1)}
), there exists
k\in I_{0}
(resp.
1\in I_{0}
), such that
\imath\neq\jmath\in\blacktriangle_{0}
(resp.
1,3,4\in\blacktriangle_{0}
) and
\{{\overline{\imath}},\overline{\jmath}\}=k
(resp.
\{\overline{1},\overline{3},\overline{4}\}=1
).
In particular, when
\mathfrak{g}=B_{n}^{(1)}
,
\jmath=2n-\imath
.
Thus to emphasize the color of
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{x}
within a fixed skeleton category, we sometimes write
\mathtt{W}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{\imath^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{x}
or
\mathtt{W}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{\jmath^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{x}
instead of
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{x}
,
according to their colors.
Throughout this paper, we specially deal with the
B_{n}^{(1)}
-cases, since
k=\{\overline{k},\overline{2n-k}\}\in I_{0}\setminus\{n\}
.

As special cases of Theorem
3.15
(
1
) and (
2
), we have the following special families of homomorphisms for classical untwisted affine types:

Corollary 3.19
.

For classical untwisted affine types
\mathfrak{g}=A_{n-1}^{(1)}
,
B_{n}^{(1)}
,
C_{n}^{(1)}
,
D_{n}^{(1)}
, and

l<n^{\prime}\mathbin{:=}n-\delta(\mathfrak{g}=D_{n}^{(1)})
, assume
1\leq a,b<n^{\prime}
with
a+b<l
. Then we have

(3.12a)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{l-b})^{-b}}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l-a}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{l-a})^{a}}\simeq\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l-a-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{l-a-b})^{a-b}}.

(a)

Consider
\mathfrak{g}=B_{n}^{(1)}
and assume
l>n
. Suppose there exists
a,b\in[1,2n-1]
such that
l-b<n<l-a
and
a+b\leq l
.
Then we have

(3.12b)

\displaystyle\mathtt{W}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q)^{-b+1}}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}\mathtt{W}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l-a}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{-(-q)^{a}}\simeq\mathtt{W}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-1)}\otimes\mathtt{W}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l-a-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q)^{a-b+1}}.

Here we understand
\mathtt{W}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l-a-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q)^{a-b+1}}
is a trivial module
\mathbf{1}
if
l=a+b
.

(b)

If
l=n^{\prime}
, we have

(3.12c)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n^{\prime}-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{n^{\prime}-b})^{-b}}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n^{\prime}-a}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{n^{\prime}-a})^{a}}

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n^{2}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-1)}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n-a-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{n-a-b})^{a-b}}&\text{ if }\mathfrak{g}=B_{n}^{(1)},\\
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n-a-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q_{\mathfrak{s}})^{a-b}}&\text{ if }\mathfrak{g}=C_{n}^{(1)},\\
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n-1}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n-1-a-b}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q)^{a-b}}&\text{ if }\mathfrak{g}=D_{n}^{(1)}.\end{cases}

We call homomorphisms in (
3.12
)
Dorey’s rule of mesh type
.
Next, let us give an example of Dorey’s rule of mesh type.

Example 3.20
.

In (
1
), we have a length
2
chain of exponents:

\{[\hskip-1.29167pt[{1,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt],[\hskip-1.29167pt[{2,3}]\hskip-1.29167pt]\}\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\{[\hskip-1.29167pt[{1,3}]\hskip-1.29167pt],[\hskip-1.29167pt[{2,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt]\}.

Then we have

\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}([\hskip-1.29167pt[{1,3}]\hskip-1.29167pt])\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}([\hskip-1.29167pt[{2,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt])\simeq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}([\hskip-1.29167pt[{2,3}]\hskip-1.29167pt])\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}([\hskip-1.29167pt[{1,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt]),

which is simple.

As special cases of Theorem
3.15
(
2
), we have the following special families of homomorphisms in (
3.13
):

Corollary 3.21
.

(a)

For
1\leq k\leq n-1
and
a\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
, there exists a
U_{q}(B_{n}^{(1)})
-module surjective homomorphism

(3.13a)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-1)^{n+1-k}aq^{-n+k}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n-k}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a(-1)^{k+1}q^{k}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n^{2}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a}.

(b)

For
1\leq k\leq n-1
and
a\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
, there exists a
U_{q}(C_{n}^{(1)})
-module surjective homomorphism

(3.13b)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a(-q_{\mathfrak{s}})^{-1-n+k}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a(-q_{\mathfrak{s}})^{n+1-k}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{2}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a}.

(c)

For
1\leq k\leq n-2
and
n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}\in\{n,n-1\}
such that
n^{\prime}-n^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{2}n-k
, there
exist a surjective homomorphism

(3.13c)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n^{\prime}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q)^{-n+l+1}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n^{\prime\prime}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q)^{n-l-1}}\to\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big).

(d)

There exists a
G_{2}^{(1)}
-module surjective homomorphism

(3.13d)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{1}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q_{\mathfrak{t}})^{-2}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{2}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-q_{\mathfrak{t}})^{5}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{2^{2}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big).

(e)

For
a\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
, there exist
U_{q}(F_{4}^{(1)})
-module surjective homomorphisms

(3.13e)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{1}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a(-q_{\mathfrak{s}})^{-5}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{1}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{a(-q_{\mathfrak{s}})^{5}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{4^{2}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{-a}\quad\text{{and}}\quad\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{2}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{-aq^{-2}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{1}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{aq_{\mathfrak{s}}^{4}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{3^{2}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{-a}.

Let us see morphisms described in (
3.13
) with examples: In (
1.22
), one can see that
there exists a unique chain of exponents satisfying the condition:

[\hskip-1.29167pt[{1,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt]\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\{[\hskip-1.29167pt[{1,3}]\hskip-1.29167pt],[\hskip-1.29167pt[{4,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt]\}\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\{[\hskip-1.29167pt[{1}]\hskip-1.29167pt],[\hskip-1.29167pt[{2,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt]\}.

Then

{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}([\hskip-1.29167pt[{2,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt])\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}([\hskip-1.29167pt[{1}]\hskip-1.29167pt]))\simeq{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}([\hskip-1.29167pt[{1,3}]\hskip-1.29167pt])\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}([\hskip-1.29167pt[{4,5}]\hskip-1.29167pt])),

which is a KR module. In (
3
), one can see also that there exists a unique chain of exponents satisfying

\langle 1,3\rangle\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\{\langle 1,4\rangle,\langle 3,-4\rangle\}\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\mathcal{Q}]}\{\langle 1,-4\rangle,\langle 3,4\rangle\}.

Then

{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 3,4\rangle)\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,-4\rangle))\simeq{\operatorname{hd}}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 3,-4\rangle)\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\langle 1,4\rangle)),

which is a KR module.

4.
Kashiwara Crystals

We recall some basic facts about Kashiwara crystals
[
Kas90
,
Kas91
]
.
Here, we give an abbreviated definition focusing on the category of crystals coming from finite dimensional (highest weight)
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
-modules in order to focus on the facts that we need, but we refer the reader to
[
BS17
,
KC02
]
for more details.

Definition 4.1
.

A
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
-crystal
(which we simply call a crystal when the quantum group is clear) is a set
\mathcal{B}
with
Kashiwara operators

\widetilde{e}_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}\sqcup\{\mathbf{0}\}
such that
\widetilde{f}_{i}b=b^{\prime}
if and only if
b=\widetilde{e}_{i}b^{\prime}
for all
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
.
We equate this with the
I_{0}
-edge labeled digraph with edges
b\xrightarrow{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}b
.
We also define statistics for each
i\in I_{0}

\varepsilon_{i}(b)=\max\{k\mid\widetilde{e}_{i}^{k}b\neq\mathbf{0}\},\qquad\qquad\varphi_{i}(b)=\max\{k\mid\widetilde{f}_{i}^{k}b\neq\mathbf{0}\},

and weight function
\mathrm{wt}\colon\mathcal{B}\to P_{0}
by
\mathrm{wt}(b)=\sum_{i\in I_{0}}(\varphi_{i}(b)-\varepsilon_{i}(b))\Uplambda_{i}
.

The direct sum of two crystals is simply their disjoint union with the obvious crystal structure.
Morphisms are the maps that commute with the crystal structure (normally these are called strict morphisms and have more restrictions than in the usual category of crystals, but it will be sufficient for our purposes).

Definition 4.2

(Tensor product of crystals)
.

For crystals
\mathcal{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathcal{B}_{k}
, we define the tensor product crystal
\mathcal{B}_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}
as the Cartesian product
\mathcal{B}_{k}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{B}_{1}
with the Kashiwara operators defined by the following
bracketing rule
.
Fix an element
b=b_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1}
and some
i\in I_{0}
.
Consider the following word of the form

\underbrace{)\cdots)}_{\varphi_{i}(b_{k})}\,\underbrace{(\cdots(}_{\varepsilon_{i}(b_{k})}\;\cdots\;\underbrace{)\cdots)}_{\varphi_{i}(b_{1})}\,\underbrace{(\cdots(}_{\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})}\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad\underbrace{)\cdots)}_{\varphi_{i}(b)}\,\underbrace{(\cdots(}_{\varepsilon_{i}(b)},

where we have done the reduction by removing all pairs ‘
()
’ from the word.
Let
m_{e}
and
n_{f}
correspond to the leftmost ‘
(
’ (resp. rightmost ‘
)
’) in the reduced word, then the Kashiwara operators are defined by

\widetilde{e}_{i}b:=b_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes(\widetilde{e}_{i}b_{m_{e}})\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1},\qquad\qquad\widetilde{f}_{i}b:=b_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes(\widetilde{f}_{i}b_{m_{f}})\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1}.

In particular,
\mathrm{wt}(b)=\sum_{m=1}^{k}\mathrm{wt}(b_{m})
.

Remark 4.3
.

We follow the tensor product convention in
[
BS17
]
, which is the opposite convention of Kashiwara
[
KC02
]
.

Let
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
denote the crystal associated to the highest weight
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
-module
V(\lambda)
, and
u_{\lambda}
the unique
highest weight element
, that is
\widetilde{e}_{i}b=\mathbf{0}
for all
i\in I_{0}
.
Kashiwara showed
[
Kas90
,
Kas91
,
Kas93
]
that the highest weight elements in a crystal correspond to the decomposition of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
-modules into simple modules (counted with multiplicity).
Furthermore, explicit tableau descriptions of
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
was given for Lie algebras of type ABCDG in
[
KN94
,
KM94
]
with all non-spin cases as follows.
Indeed, the set of
Kashiwara–Nakashima
(
KN
)
tableaux
is generated by
u_{\lambda}
being the tableau of shape
\lambda
(under the usual identification by
P_{0}\subseteq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{i}
with the coefficient of
\epsilon_{i}
being the number of boxes in the
i
-th row) with the
i
-th row filled by
i
and embedded in
\mathcal{B}(\Lambda_{1})^{\otimes\left\lvert\lambda\right\rvert}
, where
\mathcal{B}(\Lambda_{1})
is given in Figure
3
, by reading from bottom-to-top along columns and columns are read left-to-right.
A precise characterization of these tableaux are given that we omit for brevity, but we note that all KN tableaux have the following version of semistandardness.

Definition 4.4

(Semistandard tableau)
.

Let
T
be a tableaux of type
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
.
Then
T
is
semistandard
if rows (resp. columns) are weakly (resp. strictly) increasing in the alphabet
\mathcal{B}(\Uplambda_{1})
considered as a poset whose Hasse diagram is given by Figure
3
with the following exceptions:

•

In types
B_{n}
and
G_{2}
, an entry
0
cannot be repeated along rows but it can be repeated down columns.

•

In type
D_{n}
, columns do not need to be comparable (as opposed to rows).

•

In type
G_{2}
, it has more complicated rules; see
[
KM94
]
.

Figure 3.
The crystal corresponding to the natural representation
V(\Uplambda_{1})
for each of the Lie algebras of type ABCDG; here
n_{-}:=n-1
.

By the tensor product rule, we have the following well-known result.
(This is also known as the Yamanouchi condition from tableaux in type
A_{n}
.)

Proposition 4.5
.

If
b_{m}\otimes b_{m-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1}
is a highest weight element, then
b_{m-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1}
is also a highest weight element.

Theorem 4.6

(
[
Kas91
,
Kas93
]
)
.

The tensor product makes the category of highest weight crystals into a symmetric monoidal category.

An important consequence of Theorem
4.6
is that
\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}
and that a tensor product decomposes into a direct sum of highest weight crystals.

5.
Higher Dorey’s rules and generalized T-systems

In this section, we shall (i) show the homomorphisms among KR modules, called the higher Dorey’s rule, and (ii) generalize T-system by using the results have been introduced in the previous sections.
In this section, our higher Dorey’s rule will be given with some
restrictions
due to the lack of
\mathfrak{d}
-invariants among KR modules.
We will subsequently remove such restrictions by computing the
\mathfrak{d}
-invariants in the later sections.

5.1.
Preparation

In this subsection, we compute
\mathfrak{d}
-invariants and composition length of modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}

and investigate homomorphism between modules over
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, which will be used crucially in our main theorem.
Note that
\mathfrak{d}
-invariants between fundamental modules are calculated in
[
AK97
,
DO94
,
KKK15
,
Oh15
,
OS19b
]
.
We used the results several times in this subsection.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
be a quantum affine algebra of untwisted type
A_{n-1}^{(1)},B_{n}^{(1)},C_{n}^{(1)}
or
D_{n}^{(1)}
.
For
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
1\leq k,l<n
, let us assume
k+l<n-\delta(\mathfrak{g}=D_{n}^{(1)})
. Then the highest weight
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
-module
V(m\Lambda_{k+l})
appears exactly once in the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
restriction of the following modules:

(a)

\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{l})^{-k}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{k})^{l}}
,

(b)

\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{k})^{l+m-1}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m-1}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{k})^{l-1}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{l})^{-k}}
,

(c)

\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k^{m-1}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{k})^{l-1}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{l^{m-1}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{l})^{-k-1}}\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{k+l}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-\check{q}_{k+1})^{m-1}}
,

(d)

\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{(k+l)^{m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)
.

Furthermore, we have the following:

(i)

If
\mathfrak{g}=B_{n}^{(1)}
and
k+l=n
, then
V(2m\Lambda_{n})
appears exactly once in the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
restriction of the modules
(a)
\sim
(c)
and
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n^{2m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)_{(-1)}
.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{g}=C_{n}^{(1)}
and
k+l=n
, then
V(m\Lambda_{n})
appears exactly once in the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
restriction of the modules
(a)
\sim
(d)
.

(iii)

If
\mathfrak{g}=D_{n}^{(1)}
and
k+l=n-1
, then
V(m(\Lambda_{n-1}+\Lambda_{n}))
appears exactly once in the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
restriction of the modules
(a)
\sim
(c)
and
\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{n^{2m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)\otimes\mathtt{V}\big(\hskip 0.86108pt\scalebox{.8}{\boxed{(n-1)^{2m}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\big)
.

Proof.

We will prove all cases (including (i)–(iii)) by using the corresponding
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
crystals.
For (
d
), this corresponds to the dominant extremal weight, and hence appears exactly once.
From Proposition
4.5
, we know that the rightmost factor in the tensor product must be a highest weight element.
Then by weight considerations, we see there is precisely only one such highest weight element in each tensor product:

(5.1)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to14.89992pt{\vss\hbox to14.49998pt{\hss$k_{+}$\hss}\vss}\kern-14.89992pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to14.89992pt{\vss\hbox to14.49998pt{\hss$\cdots$\hss}\vss}\kern-14.89992pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to14.89992pt{\vss\hbox to14.49998pt{\hss$k_{+}$\hss}\vss}\kern-14.89992pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to14.89992pt{\vss\hbox to14.49998pt{\hss$k_{+}$\hss}\vss}\kern-14.89992pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to14.89992pt{\vss\hbox to14.49998pt{\hss$\cdots$\hss}\vss}\kern-14.89992pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\vrule height=14.89992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-14.89992pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 14.49998pt\hrule width=15.29985pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern
